@@132659556 <p> The impact of ungulates like deer in NZ is total , stretching from the mountain tops to the farm fence . Photo : Getty Images <p> Comment : Walking quietly , I try to keep my breathing under control while keeping up with Roy . He 's 13 years older than me and I 'm abashed that he 's way fitter -- too much time sitting at my desk writing submissions , I reflect . Ahead , the dog abruptly stills and stiffens . We creep closer to get a view around the bush in front of us . Eighty meters away , goats are unconcernedly grazing on the side of the track . <p> " You take the white one , I 'll take the black billy " , Roy whispers . <p> We fire almost simultaneously . Goats erupt from the vegetation , and we knock another couple over in the melee as they disperse into the thick vegetation . <h> read more <p> This is a regular activity . In the past five years we have culled 1054 goats and a considerable number of deer and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the Marlborough Sounds . During this period , the property also paid to participate in a larger landscape-scale aerial control operation spanning numerous private landowners and the Department of Conservation . That operation culled an additional 500 goats plus pigs and deer from the property . <p> While there are still animals on the property , we can see success . For the first time we are seeing Kohekohe seedlings coming up and browse damage on other broadleaf species is no longer as universal . <p> Walking hundreds of kilometres across these hills , rifle in hand , has given me plenty of time to ponder the nationwide issue of booming ungulate numbers . Booming they are . Barely a week goes by that there 's not a news story about out-of-control animal numbers . <p> People want to quantify the problem but coming up with overall numbers is n't easy . I 'd be hard pressed to estimate the remaining number of ungulates on this 600ha block . Estimating these populations nationally is next to impossible . However , from a nationwide scientific study , DoC can convincingly state @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ spread markedly , but more granular detail is difficult . <h> What are ungulates , and why are they here ? <p> ' Ungulate ' is the simple term for any mammal with hooves . In the Aotearoa New Zealand context , this refers to a rogue 's gallery of introduced pests including seven species of deer ( red , fallow , sika , rusa , wapiti , white tail and sambar ) , goats , pigs , tahr and chamois . <p> None of these animals are native . They were introduced , starting in the late 1700s , by Captain Cook ( pigs and goats ) and continuing with a vengeance in the late 19th and early 20th centuries by acclimatisation societies to establish a familiar European landscape with animals for sport . <p> A problem is that our native ecosystems , which evolved for 80 million years in the absence of browsing mammals , were ( and are ) defenceless . These introduced animals found an endless , predator-free buffet . <h> The myth of balance <p> Forest &amp; Bird warned about the dangers of high ungulate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a century of tension between conservationists and hunters . This tension is most apparent in conversations about animal numbers with hunters seeking a balance between the presence of these animals and conservation goals , and conservationists focused on ecological integrity and forest health . <p> Achieving a balance between ungulates and the environment seems reasonable . Yet it is problematic . This is because there is no balance when you step in the shoes of mother nature ; achieving a balance here and there accumulates and has ultimately resulted in severe degradation of our natural environment . <h> So why are their numbers booming now ? <p> Decades ago , government-funded professional cullers held populations in check . Later a commercial helicopter venison recovery industry further reduced numbers in many areas . But for the past 25 years government control has been minimal , and commercial aerial venison recovery is no longer profitable in most situations . <p> With mild winters ( worsened by climate change ) , abundant food , and no natural predators , these animals breed prolifically . Goats , for example , can have twins or @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is plentiful , we need to remove 25-30 percent of the population per year , just to stop the population from growing . <h> The ecological and economic damage <p> The impact of these animals is total , stretching from the mountain tops to the farm fence . <p> For our native forests , this is not just a few nibbled leaves ; it is ecosystem collapse . Ungulates are picky eaters . They target the most palatable , ' ice-cream ' species first . These include broadleaf , five-finger ( whauwhaupaku ) , kohekohe , and the seedlings of giants like totara , rata , and rimu . <p> They create what ecologists call a bovine forest . The old-growth trees remain ( for now ) , but the entire understorey -- the next generation of forest -- is eaten , along with fallen leaves from mature trees . The forest floor becomes a mown lawn of unpalatable ferns , grasses , or bare , compacted soil . <p> This destruction has flow-on effects ( a trophic cascade ) . When you remove the understorey , you remove the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ It reduces the ability of forests to absorb rainfall and contributes to downstream erosion and sediment loads . <p> For farmers this is not just a conservation issue ; it 's a primary production crisis . Deer and pigs pour out of the bush onto farmland , consuming vast quantities of high-value pasture and silage , directly competing with stock . Feral deer are a significant vector for bovine tuberculosis , posing a constant , expensive threat to our cattle and dairy herds and undermining our national disease-control efforts . Pigs root up paddocks , while deer and goats damage fences , costing farmers thousands in repairs and lost time . <h> Myths and spin <p> Recreational hunters argue that they can manage animal numbers . We value their contribution , but the evidence is clear : recreational hunters can not solve this problem on a national scale . <p> If recreational hunting could do this , it would have happened already . There are more recreational hunters than any previous time in NZ 's history . It is much easier to learn how to hunt and where to go @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Technology like GPS mapping and better optics make hunting easier and safer . Yet despite this , animal numbers are still on the rise . <p> Why ? Pest control requires maximising the kill . This means targeting all animals , especially the breeding females ( hinds , nannies ) . In contrast , many recreational hunters are seeking a trophy ( a mature stag or billy ) and will often pass up numerous animals , so they do n't scare off a possible trophy head . To allow a forest to recover ( as we 're seeing with our kohekohe ) , ungulate numbers must be reduced to very low numbers and kept there . Recreational hunting can rarely achieve this level of sustained pressure . It can usefully ' harvest ' the interest but not cull the ' principal ' . <p> Hunters routinely claim access is a barrier . Comments on a Facebook post about the South Westland article say things like : <p> " Leave it to the hunters " , and " Encourage hunter access " , and " Plenty of hunters would do @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> If access was truly the barrier , we would n't have burgeoning ungulate issues on the conservation estate where access is free and open . On private land , access is not as straightforward as people clamouring for access hope . Allowing hunters on to private land is essentially an issue of trust . A friend who bought a forestry block in Marlborough was told by the vendor that he had a trusted mate that had been doing pest control on the block and had a key for the gate . My friend decided to allow this guy to continue to hunt the block . My friend had a phone call one afternoon from the guy who said : <p> " Look , I thought I 'd better call you . My son and a few of his mates went hunting at your place . They had a fire on the vehicle track to cook up some food , and it got a bit out of control . It 's no worries though . They put it out . But I thought I 'd better let you know @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . " <p> Now , if you happen to be a pine forest owner , you are very nervous of fire . Suffice to say in response , my friend had the gate lock changed as he felt he could n't trust the guy or the people he was passing the key on to . Stories like this quickly get around landowners making trust harder to build . I think this trust issue is perhaps even greater for forestry blocks where no one is living on site . <p> Another refrain is that these animals are free food for poor families . But this meat is n't free ; the cost is just externalised . Sure , it is free meat for the hunter , or the recipient of meat but our forests , soils , and native birds pay the price for having these animals available . The idea that carcasses left out on the hill are somehow wasted is also wrong . These carcasses contribute nutrients to the forest in the same way putting blood and bone on the vege patch is beneficial . <p> Recovering carcasses from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it is prohibitively expensive , and it would be more cost effective to buy venison from a farm and give it away . The primary objective of conservation is not meat recovery . It is ecosystem protection . We must not allow our irreplaceable forests -- and the kiwi , kaka , and weta within them -- to be destroyed simply because we can not eat the pests that are killing them . <h> Legislating the problem <p> This tension between conservation and hunting is perfectly captured in our own legislation . On the one hand , the conservation legislation requires us to protect native species and halt their decline . On the other , we have the Game Animal Council Act 2013 . <p> This Act was a political compromise that formally established the Game Animal Council ( GAC ) , a government-funded hunting-lobby body . Peter Dunne of the now defunct United Future Party made it part of his coalition negotiation to reward the hunting lobby for helping get him re-elected . The Game Animal Council 's purpose is to ' manage ' certain ungulates ( deer , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ' for hunters . <p> This creates a schizophrenic , contradictory national policy . How can an animal be both a pest under the Wild Animal Control Act and a valued resource to be managed for sport ? <p> The most egregious outcome of this is the ' herds of special interest ' ( HOSI ) . The GAC can designate specific herds , and DoC is then legally required to ' manage ' them for hunting . There is currently consultation underway on draft management plans for a Wapiti HOSI in Fiordland and a Sika HOSI in the Kaweka and Kaimanawa Forest Parks . These are the first cabs off the rank and plans are afoot for a HOSI for Whitetail Deer on Rakiura Stewart Island and Himalayan Tahr in Aoraki Mt Cook , and Westland National Parks . <p> This is part of a deliberate effort by the hunting lobby to normalise these pests on the conservation estate , much as Fish &amp; Game has normalised introduced trout and salmon -- despite their devastating impact on native fish ( galaxiids ) . The hunting lobby is rebranding these @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ natural part of the landscape . <p> They are not . They are the destroyers of our landscape . Our true heritage is the kohekohe seedling , the thriving understorey , and the sound of native birds that return when these pests are controlled to a level that they are not impeding natural forest processes . <h> So , what 's wrong with herds of special interest ? <p> The main concern is the precedent they set . HOSI erode our conservation legislation -- essentially farming pest animals on the conservation estate . The upshot of allowing these to go ahead is that control of ungulates elsewhere will become more difficult . Hunters will exclaim , ' They are protected here , so why should you be controlling them there ? ' <p> We may not be as concerned about HOSI were it not for the ongoing failure of the Himalayan Tahr Control Plan . Developed in 1993 , this statutory plan was intended to cap the tahr population at 10,000 animals and prevent their spread beyond a designated feral range . However , these limits have never been achieved @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ groups , the population was allowed to balloon to more than three times the maximum allowed under the plan ( reaching nearly 35,000 in 2019 ) . Despite recent control efforts , the population remains well above the legal limit , and range expansion into exclusion zones is an ongoing crisis . <p> The Tahr Control Plan differs from HOSI in that DoC is responsible for administering it , whereas hunters administer HOSI , and DoC oversees the monitoring . However , we are not confident that this is an improvement . Instead , it keeps the government agency charged with controlling these pests at arm 's length . <p> A Wapiti or Sika HOSI might be more palatable if the draft herd management plans gave us any confidence . However , they are more akin to herd management aspirations than a plan and are ludicrously light on detail . The draft plans contain no mention of how browsing animal limits will be calculated or what sort of monitoring regime will take place to determine this . Instead , they defer this critical detail to an ' annual operational plan @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to browse our native forests will be decided year-by-year , largely outside of the public consultation process that the Herd Management Plan requires . <p> The draft plans contain little in the way of accountability and there is massive scope for fudging . They will conceivably be able to say they are achieving the goals of the plan while the health of the forest does not improve . <p> A risk that recreational hunters do n't seem to appreciate yet is that herds of special interest make their free food no longer free . Hunters will need to pay some sort of fee to hunt them as these herds are supposed to cover much of their operating costs from the hunter users . <h> What needs to happen ? <p> In recent years Forest &amp; Bird , along with farming and forestry groups , has been calling for increased ungulate control . We would like to see a national action plan for ungulate control that spans all land tenures , coordinating efforts between DoC , iwi , regional councils , landowners , and other stakeholders . Leadership for this plan @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Primary Industries , given the problem is not just on the conservation estate . <p> Just as we have national plans for wilding pines and wallabies , we need a cohesive strategy for deer , goats , and pigs . We see a national ungulate plan similarly being an enabler for regional control efforts , which could be from regional to catchment scale depending on the landowner matrix , animal presence and geography . <p> This does n't require new legislation as the Wild Animal Control Act already allows for it , but it requires ministerial direction and funding . Funding could be contestable and require contributions from landowners . We think most landowners would be happy to contribute if they were confident they were n't going to experience immediate re-invasion from a neighbour . <p> We should reject the herds of special interest . It is an ecological dead end that prioritises introduced pests over our unique natural heritage . The applications for Wapiti and Sika HOSI status should be declined . <p> Finally , we must adequately resource DoC and other agencies to implement effective , sustained control @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ecological targets . We can not rely solely on the appreciated but variable efforts of recreational hunters to protect our most vulnerable landscapes . <p> It is time to put our nature first . If you agree , join the chorus of voices calling for getting on top of runaway ungulate numbers . Our true heritage is not the trophy on the wall , but the kohekohe seedling on the forest floor . <p> That 's just not true though , is it ? Highly fecund for a feral animal , a major problem , but no need for exaggeration surely . <p> Community catchment groups are widely accepted as a way to start addressing problems of nutrient loss , native biodiversity conservation and so on . The HOSI concept is the same , except limited in application . For example , the Wapiti HOSI is not all of Fiordland , just a very limited part . And for those areas hunters accept they will have to pay -- that is exactly the point . <p> The work of the Fiordland Wapiti Foundation in the limited wapiti area is a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of over 1000 deer per year , in addition to those taken by foot hunters in the ballot over ' the bugle ' ( mating season ) . Arguably its not enough -- would Forest and Bird consider providing another $10-$20000 to increase the numbers culled ? <p> The other slight error in the opinion piece is underplaying the importance of commercial control -- Govt culling achieved at best a holding pattern until aerial commercial venison/meat recovery boomed . We have lost a lot of the capacity for commercial hunting to help us control animals ( and that control is undoubtedly needed ) through our use of toxins for other conservation work . Can we find a way around that and re-energise commercial meat recovery to make use of private sector initiative over Govt department inertia ? <p> Speaking of Government inertia -- the Tahr Control Plan is a classic example . Early monitoring suggested its set population densities might have been sustainable for native biodiversity but DOC sat on its hands , probably through lack of funding but also through a lack of willingness to deal with private land @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and Minister Sage showed that clearly ) . There are too many tahr , we need to get back to the HTCP levels and start again . <p> Thanks Scott . Good thoughts . But another item successive governments have n't cared about . Not just Peter Dunne but all of them have given away heritage and environment for votes or money . How much for a tail do you think would incentivise more non-trophy hunters to get into the bush ? ( Thanks , Google AI ) Makes one wonder if Shane Jones understands his own culture . <p> As Simon Green notes , treating a collection of people as though the collection as a whole is a person , is business as usual . Corporations , political parties , golf clubs , ... none of those have feelings or fingers , but if they have been incorporated as legal persons , then they may indeed have opinions , pay tax , type fast-track policy and submissions into their keyboards , etc . <p> Unfortunately though , Jones 's dismissal of the relevance of the legal personhood of Taranaki Maunga is not convincingly refuted by these kinds of counterexample , because Taranaki Maunga is not a collection of people , and ( bizarrely , but bear with me ) there is an interesting bit of math that implies starting with a collection of people is a structural pre-requisite for robust , coherent , incorporation of a legal @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ something as though the collection as a whole was itself one of those things , is as much business-as-usual as it is for corporate lawyers . Consider for example the infinite collection of numbers 13,37,61 , .... . That collection is not itself a number , but once incorporated ( thanks to modulo arithmetic ) as " 1pm " , it enjoys all the rights and privileges of number-hood , such as being compared and subtracted from other " numbers " , like " 2pm " . <p> So according to the math ( specifically , the theory of quotient groups ) , incorporating a coherent , useful legal person really needs to start with a collection , that includes some people . That 's OK we could expand the scope of the legal person known as Taranaki Maunga , by including some actual people , as well as the mountain . Maori culture provides the perfect mechanism for doing this , in the form of the pepeha , a process accessible to any New Zealander of any ethnicity , in which we trace our whakapapa back to our designated @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to establish our identity . But the interesting thing is , the math suggests that in doing our pepeha , we may ultimately help strengthen the identity and legal personhood of the mountain or river itself . <p> The mathematical theory of quotient groups implies another structural pre-requisite for coherent , robust legal personhood : the attributes and behaviour of the legal person must " commute " with the attributes and behaviour of its human parts . Translated into moral terms , the commutativity principle says that the privileges , duties , opinions , feelings of the legal person must at all times track those of its humans , for it is only the attributes of its human parts , that can induce human attributes in the incorporated legal fiction . Translated into political terms , commutativity implies an internally democratic governance structure for the collection of people that , along with their mountain river or species , become incorporated as a legal person . <p> Recognition of Taranaki Maunga as a legal person was a good start , but structural analysis such as the above suggests it may not @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ opportunistic and largely un-challenged cheap-shot is an example of the weakness of the current maunga . But we must , must try anything and everything we can to save what is left of the non-human world , before it is too late . Legal personhood , not just for mountains and rivers , but for each and every NZ species , does have potential , and the structural analysis provides ideas about how to make it work . <p> How about building pepeha into our constitution ? So every citizen belongs to a democratically governed group of fellow-citizens identifying with and defending a specific mountain , river or species -- their mountain , river or species . It would amount to a variation on the current system of democratic local-government , recast in units of ecological-niche rather than geographic-niche . <p> The math suggests something like universal constitutional pepeha is needed , to induce coherent , robust , effective legal personhood for mountains , rivers and species , while the dystopian long-term forecast of the consequences of business-as-usual suggests strengthening the legal rights and protections of the non-human world , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ if we are to survive . 
@@132770007 <p> Andrew Bayly still remembers the " visual assault " of Antarctica , the piercing blue clarity and scale-bending brightness . <p> The National MP tells how he once convinced his companions to join him on what he thought would be a short trip to climb a nearby peak . They borrowed skis , promised they would be back for lunch and set off . <p> Instead , the trek stretched for hours , across a crevasse field and then up a seemingly endless slope . The mountain turned out to be 12 kilometres away . <p> " We did n't get back to the base until late that night , " Bayly laughs . " It was just meant to be a little stroll . " <p> Bayly was there for a full month in 2012 , climbing mountains , including the continent 's tallest peak , Mount Vinson , and another never-before-climbed - the mountaineer 's " holy grail " . <p> " It was only three of us . We were miles from anyone , " he says . " You know that if you 're in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ More than a decade on , the landscapes have stayed with him , fuelling a personal affinity for the continent and a determination to protect it . <p> Group photo of attendees at the Antarctic Parliamentarians Assembly in Wellington . Photo : Supplied <p> In December , Bayly brought politicians , diplomats and officials from overseas to Wellington for a two-day meeting , the third Antarctic Parliamentarians Assembly . <p> Roughly 40 guests - representing nearly 20 nations - came to hear from scientists , compare notes and take home a clearer sense of what is happening at the bottom of the world . <h> From ice to influence <p> Antarctica is governed by a treaty signed in 1959 , designating it a natural reserve devoted to peace and science . It explicitly prohibits military bases , weapons testing and new territorial claims . <p> But that is not to say the continent is free from pressure . <p> Tourism has surged in recent years . More than 120,000 visitors travelled south last season , six times the numbers seen two decades ago . <p> " We want to welcome @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " Bayly says . " The question is : how do you do that in a sustainable way ? " <p> Fishing remains a concern , too . The krill fishery reached its annual catch limit this year for the first time , forcing its shutdown three months ahead of schedule - a warning sign that pressure is rising . <p> The tiny shrimp-like crustaceans are fundamental to the ecosystem as a primary food source for whales , penguins and seals . <p> Andrew Bayly at the South Pole . Photo : Supplied <p> " Certain nations really want to have a go at fishing out the krill , " Bayly says . " So , how do we protect ourselves against that ? " <p> And then there is the unmistakable effect of climate change . <p> On the Antarctic Peninsula , the area of ice-free " greening " has jumped from 86 hectares to nearly 1200 over four decades - the size of a large sheep and beef farm , now exposed land rather than ice . <p> Research teams are drilling through kilometres of ice to pull @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ than a million years . <p> " They 're going to farm out all those core samples to ... research people around the world , whoever wants them . So , you know , how do you collaborate ? " <p> Bayly says those big questions of conservation and collaboration dominated discussions among the parliamentarians , many of whom arrived with limited knowledge of Antarctica . <p> " When they go back to their home , we want them to be strong advocates ... in an informed way , " he says . <p> Among the speakers was mountaineer Peter Hillary , a moment Bayly says resonated with those visitors familiar with the legacy of his father , Sir Edmund Hillary . <p> The next assembly is already in motion , scheduled for 2027 , with Bayly asked to chair the steering committee . Several nations have already put up their hands to host : Norway , China , Italy , Argentina , and potentially more . 
@@132793664 <h> Inside the dying industry of cargo ship travel <p> Until 2020 , it was possible to book a voyage on a cargo ship . Today , it 's virtually impossible , despite being a greener , languid alternative to air travel . <p> Summer read -- originally published March 26 2025 . <p> Before the time of te Tiriti , there were few passenger ships . Crossing the Pacific in 1830 ? Usually , only a merchant could take you -- and if you did n't want to be swindled , swabbing decks , or marooned as a stowaway , you needed to hire a middleman like Hamish Jamieson . <p> Jamieson lives in Hawke 's Bay . Until 2020 , his travel agency Freighter Travel NZ served a small but dedicated group of holdouts from a bygone era of sea travel . For three decades he helped people gain passage on cargo ships -- offering slower , greener , languid alternatives to long haul flights . <p> Cost-wise , cargo travel sat between air travel and cruise ships . Jamieson could typically secure clients a cabin and three @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fee did not include anything in the way of entertainment ; passengers were left to their own devices on voyages that could last months . This , Jamieson believes , was the main appeal of cargo travel . " There was no cabaret . There were no deck chairs . There was no steward in a white coat and a simpering smile looking for a tip , " he says . " You can switch off from society . And that 's a very , very big attraction for a lot of people . " <p> Such a big attraction that Jamieson believes it was destined to explode . " Cargo travel was the fastest growing market in the world , " he exclaims . " People want to be ecologically more friendly . They want to reduce their carbon footprint . They want to de-stress . And this is the perfect opportunity . " <p> Freighter Travel NZ 's market was too small to survive the pandemic . While he insists demand was growing , driven by climate-conscious travellers and rising airfares , Jamieson 's agency closed four years @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a cargo ship as a passenger . <p> Jamieson ran his business after a lifetime with freighters , first as a purser for shipping firm P&amp;O , then as an intermediary between travellers and his merchant contacts . Before the pandemic , freighters took an average of 4,000 passengers per-year ; 29 of them booked through Jamieson in 2019 . <p> " I 'm very sad , very sad that they do n't do cargo travel any more , " says one of Jamieson 's last customers , Rania Kalogirou . The Kiwi-Greek travel writer embarked on a 45-day voyage from Singapore to Istanbul in 2019 . Her journey , she says , was eye-opening . " I got to peek behind the curtain , " says Kalogirou , recalling the industrial chaos of the seasport where she boarded her freighter . This dusty hive of cranes and checkpoints felt a world away from the tourism industry . " These are parts of the world that you do n't often get to see . " <p> Life at sea was similarly unmanicured . Kalogirou peeled potatoes in her ship 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for bartering . " You can never really get that inside experience elsewhere , " she says . " You 're part of the crew . You 're part of the ship . You feel very much like you live there . " <p> Kalogirou 's presence on board was a welcome distraction but her convenience mattered little to the merchant she sailed with . Her ship departed five days early . Then , their route changed due to conflict in Lebanon . Kalogirou also spent the entire journey without internet connection -- something she considered a blessing . <p> Beyond the sensation of living apart from the world , Jamieson believes there 's something soul-nourishing about life at sea . " Just standing by the bow , watching the world go by ... that , for me , is everything , " he says . " That 's what keeps taking me back on freighters . And I 'm absolutely gutted that at the moment we ca n't do that . " <p> Companies like Maersk and CMA C&amp;M , tightening their belts , decided passengers were more trouble @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ risk of infection , quarantine and delay . Jamieson 's network of shipping company contacts refused to take any more travellers , and a millennia-old tradition ended overnight . <p> When pandemic restrictions eased , nothing changed . Jamieson says it 's because he , and agents like him , have no leverage . For merchants , the money made by taking passengers was negligible . " I ca n't promise them more than maybe 1-1.5 million euros a year of income , " explains Jamieson , " these shipping companies make that in a week . " <p> CMA CGM and Maersk declined to comment for this story . <p> I spoke to Jamieson in early November , 2024 . Weeks later , Enzo Terranova became one of the first tourists since Covid to travel on a cargo ship . The blogger 's desperate attempts to cross the Pacific without flying made it into a French magazine , inspiring the intervention of CMA CGM executives . As it happened , the multinational CEO 's chief of staff was from Terranova 's hometown of Marseille , and executives decided to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ failed attempts to board a freighter , CMA CGM personally contacted Terranova to offer him passage from Shanghai to Los Angeles . <p> Terranova says climate change drove him to insist on cargo travel . " If I had taken the same itinerary but by plane , my carbon footprint would have been catastrophic , " he says . <p> Terranova is n't wrong : planes are the most carbon intensive form of transport . A single economy ticket travelling from Auckland to Los Angeles creates around two tonnes of emissions , whereas a cargo berth over the same distance costs just 160kg . <p> Though he is far from your average tourist , Terranova 's mindset represents a well-reported cultural shift away from planes and towards greener alternatives . Air travel accounts for 4% of all global emissions . If this seems small , consider a minuscule , wealthy number of travellers can fly . In 2024 , airlines sold 4.5 billion tickets while almost 9 billion people took trains in India alone . Moreover , unlike trains and other sectors like energy and industry , jets ca n't @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ account for 25% of our carbon budget by 2050 -- a staggering percentage given how few people fly and how the majority of these trips are for leisure . <p> In response , France recently banned thousands of short haul domestic flights . Spain is reportedly doing the same . While these laws are unlikely to curb emissions on their own , they show a growing awareness that , to fight climate change , we need to rethink how we go abroad . <p> Before Terranova crossed the Pacific , he fought for a route that was neither quick , cheap , nor easy . With media exposure , he overcame the apathy of a billion-dollar shipping firm -- showing that demand exists for slower , greener modes of travel . <h> More Reading <p> Terranova revels in his good fortune . " Every night , I sat on the deck , watching shooting stars , the infinite ocean , and I realised how lucky I was to be there , " he recalls . " For more than two months , I lived an experience outside of time . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ never get to experience . <p> " I dream that shipping companies will reopen cargo travel for passengers ... even though they 're not luxury cruises . It 's a raw , authentic , unforgettable adventure . " <p> Jamieson is happy to leave the future of container travel to advocates like Terranova . Now 75 , he 's emotionally and financially drained from years fighting for the return of his business . As good as he knows cargo travel to be , Jamieson also knows shipping companies respond to profit and not impassioned testimonies like Terranova 's . " The dreams are free , " he sighs , reclining on a La-Z-Boy with a scotch . " I 'm staying hopeful but with a tinge of , you know , that sense that you 're wrong . " <p> This summer read was brought to you with support from Tourism Fiji . With its pristine beaches and year-round tropical warmth , happiness comes naturally in Fiji . This poses problems in cheese-obsessed Italy , especially as it tries to meet growing demand for favourites like burrata . <p> As a fourth-generation cheesemaker from the Puglia region in Italy 's boot , Angelantonio Tafuno sounds in many ways like just another millennial trying to slowdown in a frenetic world . <p> On a recent fall afternoon , Tafuno , 32 , preached a more languid lifestyle for farmers and cheesemakers who " are running a little too much " . His goal , he said , was to churn out fewer @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has been making for decades , while developing specialty aged cheeses that he can produce for just a few months of the year . Behind him , a herd of Podolica cows , a breed of cattle that produces less milk than typical dairy cows , pounded toward a wooded grove to graze for the afternoon . <p> But for Tafuno , who invested in 30 head of the Podolica two years ago , the effort to develop new artisanal formulations represents more than a desire to create a gentler farm-to-table life . Doing more with less has become necessary as climate change endangers his family 's business . Extreme temperatures in Italy linked to global warming have not only contributed to dire droughts and catastrophic fires , they have also caused cows to produce less milk . <p> In Puglia -- which produces a majority of Italy 's famed creamy-centred burrata -- cheesemakers are trying to adapt to a diminishing supply of their key raw ingredient . For Tafuno , developing cheese varieties that require less milk " is a way to face what is happening now with climate @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at one of his milk suppliers last month in the southern Italian town of Altamura . Photo / Gianni Cipriano , The New York Times <p> He knows what is happening because he can feel it . He spends several mornings a week with his hands sunk deep in metal tubs of curd and whey , stirring with a large beechwood paddle to transform the consistency to taffylike lumps that he stretches and folds and ties into knots . Working with his hands , he said , he can detect whether the milk has come from stressed cows because it is thinner and takes longer to curdle . These days , he said , he feels declines in the milk 's quality more and more often . <p> The science backs him up . Depending on their sensitivity to heat and humidity and how high temperatures rise , cows ' milk production can drop anywhere from 3% to as much as 20% , said Umberto Bernabucci , a professor at the University of Tuscia who studies cow physiology . Other studies show that heat and humidity can cut milk yields @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and fat levels in milk , which directly affect the quality of cheese . <p> According to an analysis of government data by CLAL , a dairy industry research centre based in Modena , in northern Italy , milk production from cows throughout Italy has been declining because of the heat in the summer months . From 2022 to 2024 , milk production dropped an average of 17.2% between March and September , more than an average drop of 15.5% during those same months in the previous three years . <p> Dairy farmers operate on thin margins , so it does not take a drastic decrease to create what Eyal Frank , an environmental economist at the University of Chicago , calls " a milk apocalypse " . <p> At a time when restaurants in Italy teem with insatiable tourists and when exports of Italian favourites like burrata and ricotta have skyrocketed , a decline in Italy 's milk supply means some cheese producers are unable to fulfil all their orders . Although cheesemakers can import some milk , they still largely rely on local farmers . <p> Cows are @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ methane , but in this case they are victims of it . In Italy , the declining milk supply adds to a litany of problems for farmers , like rising energy and feed bills , according to Coldiretti , Italy 's largest agricultural union . A union report warns that because of these combined threats , close to one in 10 dairy farms across Italy are at risk of closing . <p> Tafuno refers to the animals owned by his milk suppliers as " my cows " . On Instagram , with luscious photos of cheese , he posts idyllic pictures of himself posing with livestock . As flies swarmed around piles of hay stacked outside pens where Jersey cows lolled in mud on a recent visit , Loiudice said she now had little choice but to invest in misters and fans . <p> " Slowly , slowly we will have to put those things up , " she said . @@132852736 <p> ' We do n't know when H5N1 might adapt to spread between people . It might be already happening , or it could happen , or it might not happen , ' virologist Jemma Geoghegan says . Photo : Getty ImagesPosted inCovid-19 <h> The next pandemic is coming . NZ is n't ready <p> Special report : The island nation is less prepared for a pandemic than it was five years ago , even as new threats emerge overseas <p> By most metrics , New Zealand trailed the rest of the world in pandemic preparedness . We had fewer ICU beds per capita than Italy , 20 District Health Boards to wrangle for any new policies -- what then-Director-General of Health Ashley Bloomfield called a " cottage industry " for contact tracing . <p> Our pandemic plan was for the wrong kind of virus and , the Royal Commission into the Covid-19 response says , it only set New Zealand up for the emergency phase , not the long haul a pandemic response actually needs . <h> read more <p> A significant amount of hard work from officials , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ force members and other frontline and essential workers went into producing what was ultimately a successful response . <p> But we got lucky too -- Covid-19 came late to New Zealand , in mid-late February rather than the middle of January . <p> The virus itself , although a far greater threat than seasonal influenza , did not kill 10 percent of its victims like the original Sars , let alone 50 percent like Ebola or H5N1 bird flu . It was infectious , but not as infectious as measles . It transmitted quickly , but not so fast that it could n't be traced . <p> There 's no time to lose , the commissioners and independent experts alike insist . Five years on from the start of Covid-19 and we 're rapidly coming up to the due date for another pandemic . Maybe we 'll luck out and it 'll be relatively minor , like the 2009 H1N1 ' swine flu ' pandemic . <p> Maybe not . <h> Royal Commission 's recommendations <p> In its report , the Royal Commission outlines six high-level lessons from the pandemic @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are to look after all aspects of people 's lives ; make good decisions ( relying on robust decision-making processes ) ; building resilience in the health system ; and also in economic and social systems ; working together with Maori , communities and the business sector ; and building the foundations of a future response . <p> The 39 recommendations are combined into six groups , which are more substantive than theoretical . The first group relates to strengthening all-of-government coordination and accountability for boosting pandemic readiness and envisages the creation of a dedicated agency function for pandemic preparedness ( and other major risks ) . <p> Group two is to ensure an all-of-government pandemic plan and response structure are developed and ready for a pandemic . These would be able to be easily activated and would be suitable for responding to a variety of pandemic scenarios . <p> ' We 're at that five years point . It was a time of incredible uncertainty , anxiety , we could see the devastation of what was happening in Northern Italy , the UK . ' <p> Ashley Bloomfield <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . The health system needs its own pandemic plan , with specific plans also in place for scaling up testing and contact tracing , implementing border restrictions and lockdowns , vaccination procurement and rollout and options ready for quarantine and isolation . <p> Group four is about pandemic planning across other sectors . In addition to preparing hospitals for the impacts of an epidemic , economic , social , justice and education sectors all need their own plans to ensure supply chains are resilient , services continue to be delivered , courts operate , prisoners ' rights are protected and schools are kept open as much as possible . <p> The fifth group looks at tools to enable the response , including improving the relationship between Maori and the Crown and reviewing pandemic legislation so it is fit for purpose . <p> Finally , group six advises the Government on how to implement all 39 recommendations . It says a minister should be placed in charge of overseeing the response to the commission with regular progress reports and a 12-month report delivered to Parliament within a year of phase one @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ wo n't be fulfilled , given the Government does n't plan to respond to the commission until next year . <h> The starting point <p> If a new pandemic were to kick off tomorrow , it would encounter a very different country than the New Zealand that responded to Covid-19 in the first months of 2020 . <p> In many ways , we are better off now than we were five years ago . The centralisation of the health system , though controversial , brings with it many benefits for coordination of a response . There 's a national contact-tracing system , rather than one siloed across 12 different public health units . ICU capacity is finally up , a bit , from where it was in 2020 . <p> But in many other ways , Covid-19 has left us worse off . The backlog of planned care is longer than ever . In September , there were more than 8000 people who had been waiting more than a year for their first specialist assessments and more than 2000 who had been waiting at least the same time for treatment @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ were seen within six hours . <p> Perhaps the greatest question that comes up when we think about a new pandemic response is one of social cohesion . The first lockdown and most of those that followed excelled in large part because people followed the rules . <p> The narrative of the ' team of five million ' was powerful and effective . After the bitterness of Auckland 's long Delta lockdown , the fury against the Covid-19 response more generally which played out in fiery scenes on Parliament 's lawn and the much-discussed pandemic fatigue , could the same spirit be captured once again ? <p> Ashley Bloomfield thinks so . <p> " We 're at that five years point . It was a time of incredible uncertainty , anxiety , we could see the devastation of what was happening in Northern Italy , the UK , " he recalls . " People sometimes ask me , would the country tolerate lockdowns ? I think it 's situation-dependent and depends on how frightened people are . " <p> Bloomfield believes the vast majority of Kiwis still trust scientists and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ loved ones and the country if it came to that . He points to a survey released in January , conducted in early 2023 , which showed New Zealand ranked ninth out of 68 countries for trust in science . <p> " I have no doubt that , once again , if we found ourselves in that situation and we were able to leverage our strategic advantage of being geographically isolated , people will look and take an objective view at that point in time . It 's hard to over-emphasise the degree of consensus there was about the use , particularly , of that first lockdown , " he says . <p> There 's other evidence to back his position too . In November 2022 , a year after the Delta lockdown and eight months after Parliament 's lawn went up in flames , a survey for the NZ Herald found nearly two in five respondents thought the Covid-19 response brought the country closer together . People were more likely to view housing access and wealth inequality as divisive than the lockdowns and mandates . <p> " Lockdowns now @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ because of the success we had with them . They wo n't always be possible or desirable , but my perspective coming out of Covid is we should be looking to do them in a wider range of circumstances than we might have otherwise , " Shaun Hendy , the Covid-19 modeller , physicist and former director of Te Punaha Matatini says . <p> " But my concern is , is the social licence there for it again ? This is my concern about the second phase of the inquiry . Is it going to build on the social licence for those tools or is it going to weaken that ? " <p> The second phase of the Royal Commission is a more narrow look at the use of lockdowns and at vaccines , including efficacy and safety . It emerged from the demands for a new or expanded Royal Commission from the Act and New Zealand First parties . <p> ' Remarkably , Taiwan managed to eliminate Covid-19 transmission in 2020 without a lockdown by mounting a rapid and highly effective public health response -- including strict border restrictions @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ face masks . ' <p> Royal Commission <p> While the science on the safety and efficacy of the Covid-19 vaccines is well-established ( and unlikely to be overturned by a single inquiry in New Zealand ) , Hendy worries anything less than a robust defence of those established facts will feed into disinformation that further weakens social cohesion . <p> " The current direction of travel -- where the Disinformation Project did n't get funded and then Joanna Kidman 's Centre of Excellence for violent extremism losing funding as well -- it does worry me . We seem to be putting our head in the sand about that stuff because that was very corrosive during the pandemic and it does seem the information environment is getting worse . That 's going to affect the social licence for public health interventions as well as things like vaccines . " <h> Missed opportunities <p> While that debate might be unresolved , there is wide agreement that part of the core of pandemic preparedness is greater investment in public health . <p> Here , too , Bloomfield says many of the improvements made @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and surveillance funding at the Institute of Environmental Science Research ( ESR ) where he is currently interim chief executive as an example . <p> " During the pandemic , ESR got a very big surge in funding . One of the good things that came out of last year 's Budget was a proportion of that -- $9 million -- was locked into ESR 's baseline budget going into the future , " he says . <p> " To put that in context , pre-Covid , the baseline health budget for ESR was about $17 million . So it 's a 50 percent increase and that is allowing ESR to maintain that wastewater surveillance and develop it into other areas , to maintain that whole genome-sequencing capability and capacity . " <p> Like lockdowns , genomics was one of the novel tools to arise during the pandemic . Jemma Geoghegan , an evolutionary virologist at the University of Otago and one of the faces of the Covid-19 sequencing effort , says it 's not a tool we can or would want to put back in the box . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , it 's just maintaining the capability and capacity of the workforce to be able to do this work is the key thing , right ? Cuts to public services and Crown Research Institutes and so on , these all affect our capacity and capability to respond to disease outbreaks and future pandemics , " she says . <p> ESR used genomics to track Covid-19 cases during the pandemic . Photo : Getty Images <p> Hendy has a personal example of what happens when governments cut funding to strategic science priorities like pandemic preparedness . When he helped found the Te Punaha Matatini Centre for Research Excellence in 2014 , infectious diseases was one of 12 focus areas . Then the centre 's funding was abruptly reduced by 40 percent before it opened and the epidemiology programme was left on the cutting room floor . <p> " We spent six years trying to find money to do some of this work and we were n't alone . There were other people out there , other teams trying to build tools , get work done , and it was pretty hard @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Punaha Matatini had been ready to run advanced disease models in January 2020 , rather than starting with more basic models in March of that year and only scaling up to the advanced ' network model ' five months later . Moreover , that same advanced model is no longer being maintained due to lack of funding and will need significant work to update it for a future pandemic . <p> Geoghegan says more infectious diseases research funding is also set to lapse this year . Te Niwha , a research platform for contagions which was given three years of support from the Strategic Science Investment Fund , will see its contract run out on June 30 . <p> " From what I know , there 's no plans for an infectious-disease platform for the next term . It does feel like the lack of funding in this area , generally , is going to be a problem for maintaining this capacity , " she says . <p> Even ESR lost 40 roles last year as some Covid-era funding lapsed , though staffing is still above pre-pandemic levels . Geoghegan @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ being eroded by the Government 's restructuring of science funding and slashing of public sector budgets . <h> Be Taiwan <p> The importance of preventative and anticipatory funding goes well beyond science and research , the Royal Commission says . While it highlights the importance in particular of a robust scenario planning and modelling capability within its recommended government pandemic readiness body , it also says more everyday public health tools need to be supported too . <p> As evidence , it points to Taiwan 's experience of the pandemic . While New Zealand fared well by almost any standards , Taiwan offers a model of how to do even better . Like us , Taiwan is an island nation with significant travel links to China , the source of the Covid-19 outbreak . They closed their border at the same time as New Zealand did and also adopted an elimination strategy -- but did so without having to lock the country down . <p> " Remarkably , Taiwan managed to eliminate Covid-19 transmission in 2020 without a lockdown by mounting a rapid and highly effective public health response -- @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ alongside widespread use of face masks , " the inquiry says . New Zealand , it suggests , could do the same thing in the future if we buff up our public health tools . <p> On this , Bloomfield believes there has been progress . <p> " I think we 're better off . We developed that national contact-tracing system which we 've still got . It can be deployed and link up the public health units across the country so you can take a national view of things , " he says . <p> " Taiwan 's a very specific example and , in a sense , both the infrastructure it had and the approach it could take was very much built on its experience with Sars . And also there are , obviously , significant cultural differences . We have to work in different ways . We could still learn from the likes of Taiwan and some other jurisdictions but we also got a lot of experience about how to deploy these interventions and capabilities in the New Zealand context . " <p> But is New Zealand @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ brush with Sars ? In November , Health New Zealand proposed cutting a quarter of the roles in the newly formed National Public Health Service . Dozens more public health jobs were proposed to be axed a week later . <p> New Zealand could have avoided lockdowns if it had better invested in public health . Photo : Getty Images <p> Hendy says this is the opposite of the approach that 's needed to actually prepare New Zealand for a pandemic . <p> " One of the reasons we had to pivot into lockdown at the last minute was that we looked in an honest way at our public health system and had to acknowledge how fragile it was , " he says . <p> " Prioritising resilience rather than just short-term cost has got to be a goal . We might be facing a virus and you 're looking at it going , ' It would be really nice to manage this without lockdowns ' . And we might just be able to do it if we had invested in the contact tracing systems , in intensive care capacity @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ year-programme of work that , if we were serious about it , might move the dial when we were considering how to respond to the next pandemic . " <p> The Treasury estimated the costs of the first lockdown to the New Zealand economy ran into the tens of billions . The Government also had to borrow billions of dollars to shore up businesses and jobs via the wage subsidy while stay-at-home orders were in place , further topping up the cost of the March lockdown . <p> If New Zealand had spent billions of dollars in the preceding years investing in public health , " it would have paid itself all back in a couple of months " through avoiding those lockdown costs , Hendy says . " Investing in that basic public health capacity will pay dividends . " <p> Then there 's the health system itself . The Royal Commission urges the Government to invest in hospital , primary and community care capacity such that many business-as-usual treatments and procedures can continue even during an epidemic . <p> Some experts back that approach , while others are @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ health system is already struggling to do the basics outside of an active pandemic . <p> " Ours was n't in a great state going into the pandemic and I do n't think we 've built a lot of capacity since the pandemic . Health has to go harder . Right now it 's in a situation where it 's struggling to deliver baseline services . We 've got to get out of that phase and get building extra capacity , " he says . <p> Bloomfield is less ambitious . He 's very aware of the issues within the health system , but says there 's no way to beef up hospital capacity to be able to handle a pandemic wave unaided . <p> " I think it 's perhaps the wrong conversation . There 's a fundamental issue about what health system capacity do we need , regardless of a pandemic , to deliver ? It 's more about models of care -- in particular , primary and community care , " he says . <p> " As we found out in the Covid pandemic , it did @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . What we did learn , though , was we could learn a lot about how you could safely continue to deliver some of that routine care even while you 're deploying those very strict measures that are protecting the health system from being overwhelmed . It 's less a matter of , ' We need to double or triple our ICU beds ' , but what are the processes that we could put in place to help ensure that we can keep delivering as much healthcare as possible . " <h> New Zealand 's CDC <p> One thing everyone can agree on is the need for some new institution or structural arrangements to oversee both pandemic preparation and potentially the response to a future health emergency as well . Unfortunately , views diverge on the specifics . <p> In its report , the Royal Commission advises the creation of a " central agency function " , to " coordinate all-of-government preparation and response planning for pandemics and other national risks " . This would n't be a standalone department , but would instead sit inside a central agency ( @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ candidate ) . <p> To ensure this work is actually happening , the Royal Commission also calls for six-monthly progress checks to be sent to ministers and a yearly report tabled in Parliament for the public to see . <p> Wendy McGuinness was one of a small cohort of New Zealanders who had thought deeply about pandemic preparedness before the onset of Covid-19 -- as early as 2006 her McGuinness Institute think tank had issued a working paper on the business risks of a pandemic . <p> " In terms of these regular reviews , I think we should also have a select committee that deals with pandemics and epidemics . They only need to meet once a year for a couple of hours and the reports are tabled and there 's an opportunity for discussion , " she says . <p> " What does that cost the country to have , versus not to have ? " <p> While McGuinness thinks the overall framework for pandemic planning outlined by the Royal Commission is well thought through , she worries the accountability mechanisms are lacking . <p> By charging just @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ministers potentially out of the loop . Opposition parties , which may be in government when the next pandemic hits , will also have to learn on the fly -- a problem that would be solved by bringing the opposition into the process via a dedicated select committee . <p> ' The way we think of earthquakes in New Zealand , we think about the magnitude and the time between events . I think this is the right way to start thinking about pandemics . They are going to come along every couple of decades . ' <p> Shaun Hendy <p> Additionally , a specific pandemic focus would ensure the response is properly run to deal with a crisis rather than an emergency . <p> " My lift pitch is an emergency is about trying to get everything back to normal . A crisis is about creating a new normal , " she explains . More specifically , emergencies are short-term , sudden events that require an immediate but ( mostly ) short-lived response -- think a cyclone or a terrorist attack . A crisis is long-term and complex -- @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ finds the government initially responded to Covid-19 like an emergency , only later realising how long the response would be and what sort of institutional arrangements would be needed . <p> For his part , Bloomfield is n't so sure about any single dedicated pandemic preparedness body . <p> " We 're a small country . Rather than focusing on structures , it 's about what are the functional capabilities we need and how do we make sure we 've got mechanisms for those , operating in a really coherent way ? The physiology rather than the anatomy , " he says . <p> Instead , Bloomfield envisages a " virtual Centre for Disease Control " which could during a pandemic coordinate the various necessary functions scattered across departments and agencies . <p> " We do n't have the capability or capacity or resources to establish lots of different bodies . Even Australia , which is establishing a CDC , it 's a big investment for them . We can work with them , we can leverage that capability , we 've got excellent relationships there . " <p> But @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's current patchwork system struggled to coordinate during Covid-19 , say something more is needed . <p> " One lesson to be learned from this is how all of these parts work together . The Ministry of Health contract ESR to do the genomics . They do the epidemiological data collection themselves or contract another part of ESR to do that , then there is another layer of information like mortality rates which may not be easily gathered in the same place , " she says . <p> " Countries that have CDCs that have the mandate to collect all of this information into one place and be able to analyse it through that lens are one step ahead of us . I think we definitely should have a central agency that deals with disease control . " <p> Another example Geoghegan reaches for is bird flu . If it arrives in New Zealand , the Ministry for Primary Industries manages the risk to poultry , the Department of Conservation focuses on wild birds and the Ministry of Health looks after human health . Tests from livestock and poultry have @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ lab , while human samples can be tested much more quickly by a variety of services . <p> " There 's so many government agencies at play here that would need to be involved in this response . They do n't necessarily have the ability to share data and communicate in that way that they can make informed decisions . If one central agency was responsible for disease control , that would lower those boundaries between these different siloed agencies . " <p> Rangers clear bird carcasses from the site of an H5N1 outbreak in England . Photo : Getty Images <p> In the early days of the Covid-19 response , Hendy saw these issues firsthand . Although Te Punaha Matatini was providing modelling to inform the government 's decision-making , they were unable to get access to case data . Instead , Hendy watched the daily 1pm press conference and manually entered case counts and locations into a spreadsheet to feed it into the model . <p> " The response could have been more effective if instead of having to learn on the job , you had people who @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have . The first month of my involvement in the response , half my time was spent building connections and trying to find who did what , " he recalls . " If we had n't had to built that on the fly , I think there are a lot of things that could have been done better early on . " <p> This flows on to another key part of preparedness : planning . Bloomfield is quick to specify that planning is different from a plan . <p> " We ca n't go down that same road of feeling that we 've got a plan so we 're ready . It 's actually thinking about planning , not a plan , " he says . <p> " It 's about taking that more dynamic approach . That 's what served us well during Covid . It was n't about the plan because actually the plan was for the wrong virus . It was about the ongoing agility , planning , thinking . " <p> A similar theme comes up in McGuinness ' comments , as she says the entire @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ wrong order . <p> " Officials should actually be continuously working towards delivering New Zealand the best protection and the Royal Commission report should then come in after that and be able to comment on how much progress we 've made and where the gaps lie , " she argues . <p> " Why would we delay and create what I would call an operational gap when we do n't have to ? Why would n't we use the Royal Commission of Inquiry phase two as an ability to improve on our already-improved plan . " <p> The inquiry recommends national pandemic response exercises be held at least once every three years , with the pandemic plan to be updated after each . It 's an idea Bloomfield believes should begin right now . <p> " At the moment , the thing we 're all watching and we 're worried about is highly pathogenic avian influenza , " he says . " I would have thought sometime this year we need to do a pretty serious exercise : Let 's say that developed into the next pandemic pathogen . And @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ learned , what else do we need to do ? " <h> The next pandemic <p> Looming above any discussion about pandemic readiness , there is an elephant seal in the room : the H5N1 strain of avian influenza , which has crossed into mammals in dairy herds in the United States and seal colonies in Argentina . <p> In one outbreak in elephant seals in 2023 , an estimated 17,000 individuals were killed by the virus , including 97 percent of all pups . Researchers concluded late last year that the H5N1 strain had adapted to spread between at least these mammals -- something it had not previously been able to do , and a critical step on the path to pandemic potential . <p> While there 's no evidence yet of human-to-human transmission , hundreds of people have been infected with H5N1 by animals over the years since the strain 's emergence in the mid-1990s . Half of them have died . <p> Hendy points to one of the appendices of the Royal Commission report , in which the inquiry describes the estimated " return period " for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to see each type . Its data suggests less virulent ( lethal ) pandemics that would without response measures kill 15,000 to 30,000 New Zealanders should be expected once every 25 years . <p> Higher-virulence pandemics , which could kill hundreds of thousands without response measures , are closer to once in a century . <p> " The way we think of earthquakes in New Zealand , we think about the magnitude and the time between events . I think this is the right way to start thinking about pandemics . They are going to come along every couple of decades , " Hendy says . <p> " We should start investing if we 're going to be having another pandemic similar to Covid-19 in 10 to 20 years . The thing that makes it different to earthquakes is that we actually know that the risks are increasing over time . Empirically , humans are exposing themselves to more and more potentially pandemic-causing viruses . That 's been happening over the last few decades as we encroach into natural habitat and climate change is going to accelerate this . " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that it seems the Government does not share . Internal Affairs Minister Brooke van Velden has said Cabinet will wait until February , when the second phase of the Royal Commission reports back on its narrower remit relating to lockdowns and vaccine efficacy and safety , before considering the recommendations of the broader first phase . <p> While some agencies are advancing recommendations that pertain to them , this is being done in a piecemeal fashion . The most critical changes , like a new agency function for preparedness and coordinated cross-government work , require Cabinet approval which wo n't be forthcoming for months . <p> " I do n't think that can be left till February 2026 , " McGuinness says . " There 's nothing new that 's really going to come out from the second report . " <p> The longer the Government waits , the further behind New Zealand will be in the process of continuous improvement . Australia is already setting up a CDC . The United Kingdom reformed its public health agency into the Health Security Agency as early as 2021 . <p> And @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ overseas . <p> " We do n't know when it might adapt to spread between people . It might be already happening , or it could happen , or it might not happen , " Geoghegan says . <p> " We know viruses adapt rapidly and it 's already spreading in mammals and cows . Just waiting for that to become a human problem is going to result in quite a lot of failure .
