@@132882034 <p> Academic Quentin Atkinson says a year without flying meant more time with family and more connection to places he cared about . Photo : David Williams <p> For a country that prides itself on being clean and green , Aotearoa has an outsized problem in the skies . <p> Flying accounts for a notable chunk of New Zealand 's gross carbon emissions -- around 12 percent before the Covid-19 pandemic and well above the global per-capita average -- driven by long distances , a tourism-heavy economy and far-flung families . <p> It 's a climate challenge that is n't getting any easier . Research shows our aviation emissions more than doubled between 1990 and 2019 , and that even optimistic assumptions about sustainable aviation fuels and new aircraft would still see us fall short of our fair share of a Paris-aligned carbon budget unless we also fly less . <p> Six years on , as airlines retreat from near-term climate targets and policy momentum falters , we ask what they learned , and what people can realistically do now . <p> In 2018 , you both took a year @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ aviation emissions to actually committing ? And , looking back , what did that year teach you about work , family life and your own relationship with climate anxiety ? <p> Shaun Hendy ( SH ) : For me , the immediate motivation was the first Trump administration . This was early 2017 , just after Donald Trump had become president and was talking about withdrawing the US from the Paris Agreement . I remember feeling pretty helpless . I 'm not a climate scientist , but I follow the science closely and had done a lot of communication around it , and I was thinking : what can I actually do ? Around that time , I happened to be at a talk Quentin was giving about costly signalling , and the two things sort of came together . I thought this might have enough novelty to cut through , not just as a personal choice , but as a science communication tool . At the same time , I was very aware that , as an academic , the biggest chunk of my own personal emissions came from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ credibility : what does it mean to stand up and tell people we need to act on climate change , while not really making a dent in your own emissions ? <p> Quentin Atkinson ( QA ) : I 'd been concerned about climate change for a long time , and if you 're concerned about climate change , you inevitably start thinking about your own behaviour and what you do with your money . There 's really no more efficient way of turning money into carbon emissions than buying plane tickets . So , flying seemed like an obvious behaviour to target , especially because not all of the travel we do is actually essential . I 'm a psychologist who studies climate behaviour , and one of the things I 'm interested in is the gap between what people say and what they do . From my work on religion , I know that actions often speak as loudly as , or louder than , words . So , the idea was to take a credible action -- a " costly signal " -- that showed this was @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ turned out not to be as costly as I 'd expected . A year without flying meant more time with family , more connection to places in New Zealand that I already cared about . It was genuinely enjoyable . <p> Six years on , do you think what you did genuinely shifted awareness or behaviour among colleagues and the wider public ? <p> SH : Anecdotally , it does seem to have made a difference , at least within academia . I know a lot of colleagues who 've told me they 've changed how they travel or at least thought more carefully about it . Even now , when I see people at conferences , one of the first things they 'll do is tell me how they got there or ask me how I travelled . As for the wider public , it may well have been preaching to the converted . <p> QA : With costly signalling , it 's not enough just to do the costly thing : you also have to do the signalling . Shaun did that well : interviews , writing , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to me . Someone will email about a meeting and say , " I know how you feel about flying . " So clearly it lodged somewhere . <p> Many people want to reduce their flying but ca n't realistically stop altogether -- especially academics , consultants and people with family offshore . In 2026 , is a no-fly year still useful , or is a " flight diet " -- fewer , longer , better-planned trips -- the more practical path ? <p> SH : I definitely think a flight diet is something people should be thinking about . I now take fewer , longer trips and plan them much more carefully . I largely do n't do day trips anymore . Instead , I 'll block out a full week in Wellington and it does n't take long for that week to fill up with meetings . So yes , the flight diet makes a lot of sense . If we had n't had the pandemic , I 'd still be recommending a no-fly year as a kind of detox . There were real benefits . <p> QA @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be rare , with longer , better-planned trips . A flight diet is clearly better than changing nothing . At the same time , I have mixed feelings . In some ways I think it 's actually harder to fly " a bit " than to just cut it out altogether . You 're constantly evaluating whether a trip is justified . If you just say no , it can be oddly liberating . Given where we are with CO2 levels , it 's hard to justify most flights unless you really need to do them . <p> If someone is considering drastically cutting their flying in 2026 , what are the biggest benefits you 'd point to , and what are the main traps or realities they need to be prepared for ? <p> SH : One of the big benefits is efficiency . You can organise your time much better and often spend less on travel overall . I think I spent more time with my family that year than in the year before . One of the traps now is infrastructure . Public transport in New Zealand @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ worse . That makes avoiding flights harder . Electric vehicles and car-share schemes have improved a lot , though . The worst thing you can do is replace flying with driving an internal combustion car : in that case , driving is worse than flying . But EVs make substitution much easier . <p> QA : You discover amazing places more locally , spend less time in airports , and feel more connected to where you are . There are downsides . For academics , it might mean missing certain opportunities . For business people , where travel is tied to livelihood , it 's harder . But even then , there are opportunities to be more judicious about when and how you travel . <p> University of Auckland academic Professor Quentin Atkinson decided not to fly for a year as an example-setting exercise in ' costly signalling ' . Photo : Supplied <p> A lot of hope is pinned on sustainable aviation fuels , electric or hydrogen aircraft , and efficiency gains . But research suggests New Zealand would still blow through its fair share of a Paris-aligned carbon @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , what would an honest national aviation plan actually look like -- and what does Air New Zealand 's recent retreat from its 2030 climate target tell us about the limits of voluntary , technology-led solutions ? <p> SH : Aviation emissions are " hard-to-avoid " emissions , meaning there 's no straightforward technological fix or substitute , especially for long-haul travel . That means reducing how often we fly is crucial . We ca n't eliminate flying altogether -- much international travel is about seeing family -- and any honest plan has to acknowledge that . Domestically , we could invest far more in making lower-carbon travel attractive . On the airline side , I do n't think we 'll see sufficient change voluntarily . Aviation is highly competitive , and that 's why we need national and international policy . Air New Zealand stepping back from its science-based target reflects reality . Offsets can only take you so far , and efficiency gains can backfire by increasing demand . Technology will help at the margins , but it wo n't rescue us from having to fly less . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's decision mostly confirmed my suspicions . It shows this is n't something the industry is going to solve voluntarily . That 's disappointing , but it should also be clarifying . If airlines are n't going to tackle this themselves , then national and international regulation becomes unavoidable . There 's a whole body of research looking at how individual actions and technological promises can be used -- sometimes unintentionally -- to delay or deflect systemic change . That does n't mean individual behaviour does n't matter , but it does mean we need to be very clear about what actually drives change . Without strong , coordinated policy , especially in aviation , we 're not going to get anywhere near the scale of change that 's required . <p> How should we think about fairness ? Is it reasonable to keep focusing on individual choices when the aviation system still assumes constant growth , and when most emissions come from a relatively small number of frequent flyers ? <p> SH : A very small percentage of the population is responsible for a large share of emissions , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , this is a problem for the one-percenters . Messaging about individual action is most relevant to people in that high-emissions group , who often have more flexibility . Individual action also sends a signal to politicians that people care . Despite this , as a country we 've stepped back from climate leadership . That 's not the kind of country I want to live in . I want to see political leadership that shows what 's possible . <p> In 2018 , University of Auckland academic Professor Quentin Atkinson opted to take the train over planes . Photo : Quentin Atkinson <p> For people who still need to fly , what are the most useful tools or choices -- from offsets to route selection , class of travel , emissions calculators or apps -- that actually make a measurable difference ? And what should people be wary of ? <p> SH : There 's no substitute for flying less . Offsets help but need to be treated cautiously . You have to ask whether the trees planted will last for centuries and account for the higher warming impact @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ genuinely eye-opening . Smaller choices , like using electric public transport to get to the airport , wo n't cut much from total emissions , but they do make you more mindful . <p> QA : Offsets should n't be an excuse to fly more . If you do offset , approaches that reduce emissions at source feel more robust than relying entirely on future tree growth . Ultimately , these tools should n't distract from the bigger task . Individual choices matter , but without pressure on policymakers , their impact will always be limited . <p> The world is facing unprecedented environmental challenges . Planetary Solutions , an initiative of the Sustainability Hub at Waipapa Taumata Rau , University of Auckland , and Newsroom , explores these issues -- and the practical ways we can all be part of the solution . <h> Join the Conversation <p> Let 's ditch the platitudes and trivia . If we want to stabilise the climate , we primates need to get our annual per capita GHG footprint down to around 2 tonnes CO2e . Currently , the NZ figure is around @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ) . Either we give a damn about our kids ' future or we do n't ; anyone who is not actively striving to get down to 2 tonnes clearly does n't care . That 's deeply confronting , and when it comes up in conversation most people prefer to change the subject . The most effective action of course is at the political level ; but let 's face it , the distinction between individual , corporate and government responsibility is utterly artificial . For most citizens , the quickest way to cut emissions is to ditch non-essential fossil-fuelled travel . I doubt I will ever fly again -- it 's the least I can do , but it 's a bitter pill to swallow as all my family are in the UK and Canada , and I 'd love to revisit East Africa where I grew up and still have many friends . Arguing about whether flying is better or worse than driving seems a bit trivial ; we face a global , urgent , existential crisis and collectively we are failing . Either the 2 tonne challenge @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Our World in Data suggests a domestic flight emits about 246 grams of CO2 per passenger kilometer . At the other end of the scale is a Eurostar electric train emitting just 4 grams . As Graham says getting down to 2 tonnes per person means we have to invest in the most energy efficient , least carbon emitting ways of travelling . Which , domestically , means electrified rail . Challenging in a country with sea between the two main islands if you want a weekend partying in Queenstown travelling from Auckland . But in our most populated area of NZ , Auckland , Hamilton and Tauranga , electrified rail could replace many car and plane trips . A night train between Auckland and Wellington would reduce flights . Internationally , we need action to make sure flights pay their full cost of damage . Currently you do not even pay GST if flying internationally , including any domestic leg if using a plane for that ..

@@132900531 <p> In 2025 , children were deeply affected by climate disasters around the world -- from heatwaves that forced schools to close to flash floods and storms that flattened infrastructure and pushed children and families to live in temporary shelters . <p> Save the Children data this year showed about 136,000 children a day have been affected by climate disasters over the past 30 years , highlighting the need for decisive action to protect children against the impacts of climate change . <p> Two million children would avoid living with unprecedented lifetime exposure to droughts if we can collectively reach the Paris Agreement 's goal of limiting global warming to 1.5 ? C by 2100 2 <p> Here are five times in 2025 when climate disasters disrupted children 's lives . <p> Asia floods : In late 2025 devastating floods left hundreds of people , including children , dead . The floods -- some of the worst in a generation in some countries -- forced schools to close , leaving tens of thousands of children out of education , including in flooded areas of Thailand , Indonesia , Sri Lanka @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Save the Children and local partners created safe spaces for children where they could play , learn and recover . We also delivered essential aid to affected families . <p> The strongest hurricane on record in 2025 : Hurricane Melissa unleashed devastating winds and torrential rain across Haiti and the Dominican Republic . According to the Imperial College Storm Model ( IRIS ) , climate change increased the extreme rainfall associated with Melissa by 16%. 1 Save the Children launched emergency responses in Haiti and the Dominican Republic to support children in the hardest-hit areas . Here are 10 Hurricane Safety Tips for families and children . <p> Dangerous heat forces schools to shut in South Sudan : In February , dangerously high temperatures forced schools across South Sudan to close for the second year in a row , putting learning out of reach for many children and pushing them further into risks like early marriage , child labour and recruitment into armed groups . This also highlighted the severe impact of heatwaves on children studying in schools with no air conditioning and poor ventilation . <p> Malnutrition in Madagascar @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ caused by cyclones , contributed to agricultural losses this year and cases of malnutrition among children under five are now expected to increase by 54% in Madagascar in the coming months , according to an analysis by Save the Children . Food insecurity in Madagascar is the result of several factors , including recurring climate shocks . 3 . <p> Persistent storms in the Philippines : Children in the Philippines were hit by 23 tropical cyclones this year 4 , with several studies showing a relationship between rising ocean temperatures and increasing typhoon intensity . Typhoon Kalmaegi , which battered the Philippines in November , killed about 200 people , including babies and children , and affected areas of the country that were already suffering from the impact of a 6.9 magnitude earthquake that struck south-central Philippines . " Just when they 're about ready to start recovery , another disaster arrives , closing schools and displacing communities , " Faisah Ali , Humanitarian Manager , Save the Children Philippines , said at the time . <h> 2 COMMENTS <p> While responsible leaders preach to the converted about Climate changes @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ who are not interested in anything that is happening unless it 's about them and they 're OK .
